Codex · Sep 8, 2026 · 5 prompts · 15 replies · #41

Sep 8, 2026, 12:15 UTC

Davis

In the current PR, there are comments on GitHub about a performance benchmark

Rerun those benchmarks against main to confirm that they still hold (or maybe have even improved)

Also do your own thinking about other benchmarks we may want to run here, and go ahead and run them against main.

Post your results to GitHub as another comment that we can use as a before/after comparison for this PR with a full reprex of the benchmark code

Codex

I’ll identify the current PR and its existing benchmark comments, reproduce them on both the PR branch and main, add a few targeted benchmarks of my own, then post one GitHub comment containing the results and fully runnable benchmark code. I’ll keep the working tree untouched while switching revisions by using temporary worktrees or equivalent isolated checkouts.

{"baseRefName":"main","body":"","comments":[{"id":"IC_kwDOUHAoJs8AAAABS4qt-w","author":{"login":"DavisVaughan"},"authorAssociation":"OWNER","body":"## Grid benchmark: does the runs optimization only help axis 1?\n\nShort answer: **no**. Swept `rray_add()` across vector size, element type, operation (runs vs. non-runs), and which axis is being broadcast. Every configuration benefits, and the win isn't concentrated on any one axis.\n\n### Part A — shape × broadcast axis (5M `dbl` + `dbl`)\n\n`tall` = large axis 0 / small axis 1 (long runs, few carries). `wide` = small axis 0 / large axis 1 (short runs, many carries) — the shape built specifically to stress the carry path.\n\n| shape | broadcast | ns/element |\n|---|---|---|\n| wide | axis0 | 0.788 |\n| vector | scalar | 0.750 |\n| tall | axis0 | 0.747 |\n| wide | none | 0.743 |\n| tall | none | 0.724 |\n| vector | identity (no broadcast) | 0.717 |\n| tall | axis1 | 0.706 |\n| wide | axis1 | 0.656 |\n\nWorst to best is only a ~1.2x spread. The shape purpose-built to maximize axis-1 carries (`wide`) isn't meaningfully worse than the shape that minimizes them (`tall`).\n\n### Part B — `add()` [runs] vs `subtract()` [no runs], identical shapes\n\n| shape | broadcast | add (ns/elt) | subtract (ns/elt) | speedup |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| tall | axis0 | 0.759 | 2.208 | 2.91x |\n| vector | identity | 0.738 | 2.210 | 3.00x |\n| tall | none | 0.731 | 2.283 | 3.12x |\n| vector | scalar | 0.710 | 2.237 | 3.15x |\n| wide | axis0 | 0.749 | 2.369 | 3.16x |\n| tall | axis1 | 0.689 | 2.275 | 3.30x |\n| wide | none | 0.718 | 2.395 | 3.34x |\n| wide | axis1 | 0.649 | 2.379 | 3.66x |\n\nEvery shape/broadcast combination gets a solid win (2.9x–3.7x). There's no configuration where the runs version regresses relative to the non-runs baseline.\n\n### Part C — type sweep, at the fastest and slowest shapes from Part A\n\n| shape | broadcast | type | ns/element |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| wide | axis0 | lgl+lgl | 1.108 |\n| wide | axis0 | int+int | 1.073 |\n| wide | axis0 | int+dbl | 0.890 |\n| wide | axis0 | dbl+dbl | 0.750 |\n| wide | axis0 | cpl+cpl | 1.347 |\n| wide | axis1 | lgl+lgl | 1.111 |\n| wide | axis1 | int+int | 1.098 |\n| wide | axis1 | int+dbl | 0.682 |\n| wide | axis1 | dbl+dbl | 0.645 |\n| wide | axis1 | cpl+cpl | 1.341 |\n\nType matters more than shape does. `dbl+dbl` is fastest, `cpl+cpl` slowest (16 bytes/element, as expected). Side finding, unrelated to the runs work: `int+int` is *slower* than `dbl+dbl` despite half the memory footprint — `rray_add_int_one()` runs an overflow check on every element, and `int+dbl` (which promotes to double and skips that branch) is measurably faster than pure `int+int`. Worth keeping in mind if `subtract`/`multiply`/`divide`/`exponentiate` get the runs treatment later.\n\n### Part D — size sweep at the worst shape from Part A (`wide`, axis0, `dbl`+`dbl`)\n\n| size | ns/element |\n|---|---|\n| 2e5 | 0.837 |\n| 1e6 | 0.787 |\n| 4e6 | 0.731 |\n| 2e7 | 1.256 |\n\nSmall sizes pay more fixed per-call overhead; very large sizes (2e7 elements = 160MB per array) hit real memory-bandwidth limits. Neither is specific to the runs design — any implementation hits this curve eventually.\n\n### Conclusion\n\nThe runs optimization isn't axis-1-specific. Absolute speed is driven far more by element type (double vs. int vs. complex) and total data volume (cache/bandwidth) than by which axis is broadcast or how many carries a shape forces. Non-runs `subtract()` is consistently ~3–4x slower than runs-based `add()` across the entire grid, with no weak points found.\n\n<details>\n<summary>Benchmark script</summary>\n\n```r\ndevtools::load_all(quiet = TRUE)\n\nmake_arr <- function(type, dim) {\n  n <- prod(dim)\n  arr <- switch(\n    type,\n    lgl = rep(c(TRUE, FALSE), length.out = n),\n    int = as.integer(seq_len(n) %% 1000L),\n    dbl = as.double(seq_len(n) %% 1000L),\n    cpl = complex(real = seq_len(n) %% 1000L, imaginary = 1)\n  )\n  array(arr, dim = dim)\n}\n\nbuild_case <- function(size, shape, bcast, type_x, type_y) {\n  if (shape == \"vector\") {\n    dim_x <- size\n    dim_y <- switch(bcast, identity = size, scalar = 1)\n  } else {\n    if (shape == \"tall\") {\n      d0 <- size %/% 100\n      d1 <- 100\n    } else if (shape == \"wide\") {\n      d0 <- 100\n      d1 <- size %/% 100\n    }\n    dim_x <- c(d0, d1)\n    dim_y <- switch(\n      bcast,\n      none = c(d0, d1),\n      axis0 = c(1, d1),\n      axis1 = c(d0, 1)\n    )\n  }\n  list(\n    x = make_arr(type_x, dim_x),\n    y = make_arr(type_y, dim_y),\n    size = prod(dim_x)\n  )\n}\n\ntime_ns_per_element <- function(fn, size, iterations = 20) {\n  gc()\n  t <- bench::mark(fn(), iterations = iterations, check = FALSE, memory = FALSE)\n  as.numeric(t$median) * 1e9 / size\n}\n\ncases <- rbind(\n  expand.grid(\n    shape = \"vector\",\n    bcast = c(\"identity\", \"scalar\"),\n    stringsAsFactors = FALSE\n  ),\n  expand.grid(\n    shape = c(\"tall\", \"wide\"),\n    bcast = c(\"none\", \"axis0\", \"axis1\"),\n    stringsAsFactors = FALSE\n  )\n)\n\ncat(\"=========================================================\\n\")\ncat(\"Part A: shape x broadcast axis, size = 4e6, dbl + dbl\\n\")\ncat(\"=========================================================\\n\")\n\nresA <- data.frame()\nfor (i in seq_len(nrow(cases))) {\n  shape <- cases$shape[i]\n  bcast <- cases$bcast[i]\n  case <- build_case(4e6, shape, bcast, \"dbl\", \"dbl\")\n  ns <- time_ns_per_element(function() rray_add(case$x, case$y), case$size)\n  resA <- rbind(\n    resA,\n    data.frame(shape = shape, bcast = bcast, ns_per_elt = ns)\n  )\n}\nresA <- resA[order(-resA$ns_per_elt), ]\nprint(resA, row.names = FALSE)\n\ncat(\"\\n=========================================================\\n\")\ncat(\"Part B: add() [runs] vs subtract() [no runs], same case <- build_case(4e6, shape, bcast, \"dbl\", \"dbl\")\n  ns_add <- time_ns_per_element(function() rray_add(case$x, case$y), case$size)\n  ns_sub <-\n    time_ns_per_element(function() #' Broadcast an array to new dimensions
#'
#' @description
#' `rray_broadcast()` broadcasts an array to a new set of dimensions using
#' tidyverse recycling rules. Each dimension of `x` must either match the
#' corresponding target dimension or be 1, in which case it is repeated to fill
#' the target.
#'
#' Dimensionality can be expanded by supplying `dimensions` with greater
#' dimensionality than `x` has. For example, a 2x3 array can be broadcast to a
#' 2x3x4 array.
#'
#' @param x An array.
#' @param dimensions An integer vector of target dimensions.
#'
#' @returns
#' An array with dimensions of `dimensions`.
#'
#' @export
#' @examples
#' # Broadcast a vector to a matrix
#' rray_broadcast(1:3, c(3L, 2L))
#'
#' # Broadcast a row to fill a matrix
#' rray_broadcast(array(1:2, c(1L, 2L)), c(3L, 2L))
#'
#' # Add a new dimension
#' rray_broadcast(array(1:6, c(2L, 3L)), c(2L, 3L, 4L))
rray_broadcast <- function(x, dimensions) {
  .Call(ffi_rray_broadcast, x, dimensions, environment())
}

#' Broadcast arrays to common dimensions
#'
#' @description
#' `rray_broadcast_common()` broadcasts every array to the common dimensions
#' found by [rray_dimensions_common()].
#'
#' @param ... Arrays to broadcast.
#'
#' @param .dimensions If provided, an integer vector of dimensions to
#'   broadcast to, rather than computing common dimensions from `...`.
#'
#' @returns
#' A list of arrays, all with the common dimensions.
#'
#' @export
#' @examples
#' rray_broadcast_common(array(1:3, c(3L, 1L)), array(1:2, c(1L, 2L)))
#'
#' # Names of `...` are kept
#' rray_broadcast_common(x = 1:3, y = 1L)
#'
#' # `.dimensions` overrides the common dimensions
#' rray_broadcast_common(1:3, .dimensions = c(3L, 2L))
rray_broadcast_common <- function(..., .dimensions = NULL) {
  .Call(ffi_rray_broadcast_common, list2(...), .dimensions, environment())
}
#' Split an array along axes
#'
#' @description
#' `rray_split()` splits `x` along the specified `axes`, returning a
#' list of subarrays. The dimensionality of `x` is retained in each
#' subarray, with the split axes collapsed to a dimension of 1.
#'
#' @param x An array.
#'
#' @param axes An integer vector of axes to split along. The number of resulting
#'   subarrays is the product of the dimensions along `axes`.
#'
#' @returns
#' A list of arrays, each with the same dimensionality as `x` but
#' with a dimension of 1 along the split `axes`.
#'
#' @export
#' @examples
#' x <- array(1:24, c(4, 3, 2))
#'
#' # Split along the 3rd axis
#' # (4, 3, 2) -> two (4, 3, 1) arrays
#' rray_split(x, 3)
#'
#' # Split along the 1st axis
#' # (4, 3, 2) -> four (1, 3, 2) arrays
#' rray_split(x, 1)
#'
#' # Split along multiple axes
#' # (4, 3, 2) -> twelve (1, 1, 2) arrays
#' rray_split(x, c(1, 2))
rray_split <- function(x, axes) {
  .Call(ffi_rray_split, x, axes, environment())
}
#' Reduce an array along axes
#'
#' @description
#' - `rray_sum()` computes the sum along the specified `axes`.
#'
#' - `rray_prod()` computes the product along the specified `axes`.
#'
#' @details
#' The dimensionality of `x` is retained in the result, with the reduced axes
#' collapsed to size 1.
#'
#' If summing an integer array would overflow, an error is thrown.
#'
#' @section Casting:
#' Certain inputs are upcast, changing the return type:
#'
#' - `rray_prod()`: logicals and integers are cast to double.
#'
#' @param x An array.
#'
#' @param axes An integer vector of axes to reduce over. `1` reduces
#'   rows, `2` reduces columns, and so on.
#'
#' @param ... These dots are for future extensions and must be empty.
#'
#' @param na_rm If `TRUE`, missing values are removed before reducing.
#'
#' @returns
#' An array with the same dimensionality as `x`, but with the dimensions along
#' `axes` reduced to 1.
#'
#' @name reduce
#' @examples
#' x <- array(1:10, c(5L, 2L))
#'
#' # Sum along rows
#' rray_sum(x, 1L)
#'
#' # Sum along columns
#' rray_sum(x, 2L)
#'
#' # Sum along both axes
#' rray_sum(x, c(1L, 2L))
#'
#' # Product along rows
#' rray_prod(x, 1L)
NULL

#' @rdname reduce
#' @export
rray_sum <- function(x, axes, ..., na_rm = FALSE) {
  check_dots_empty0(...)
  .Call(ffi_rray_sum, x, axes, na_rm, environment())
}

#' @rdname reduce
#' @export
rray_prod <- function(x, axes, ..., na_rm = FALSE) {
  check_dots_empty0(...)
  .Call(ffi_rray_prod, x, axes, na_rm, environment())
}

#include "split.h"

#include "axes.h"

r_obj* ffi_rray_split(r_obj* ffi_x, r_obj* ffi_axes, r_obj* ffi_frame) {
  struct r_lazy error_call = {.x = ffi_frame, .env = r_null};
  return rray_split(ffi_x, ffi_axes, rray_args.x, error_call);
}

r_obj* rray_split(
  r_obj* x,
  r_obj* axes,
  struct rray_arg* arg,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  check_unclassed(x, arg, error_call);
  x = KEEP(arg_as_array(x, arg, error_call));

  r_obj* x_dimensions = KEEP(rray_dimensions(x, arg, error_call));
  const int* v_x_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(x_dimensions);

  const int dimensionality = rray_dimensionality_from_dimensions(x_dimensions);

  axes = KEEP(arg_as_axes(axes, dimensionality, rray_args.axes, error_call));
  const int* v_axes = r_int_cbegin(axes);
  const r_ssize axes_size = r_length(axes);

  // Splitting 4x3x2 on axis 3 gives 4x3x1 out dimensions
  r_obj* out_elt_dimensions = KEEP(rray_set_axes_dimension(
    v_x_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    v_axes,
    axes_size,
    1
  ));
  const int* v_out_elt_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(out_elt_dimensions);

  r_obj* axes_complement =
    KEEP(rray_axes_complement(v_axes, axes_size, dimensionality));
  const int* v_axes_complement = r_int_cbegin(axes_complement);
  const r_ssize axes_complement_size = r_length(axes_complement);

  // Splitting 4x3x2 on axis 3 gives 1x1x2 split dimensions
  r_obj* out_dimensions = KEEP(rray_set_axes_dimension(
    v_x_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    v_axes_complement,
    axes_complement_size,
    1
  ));
  const int* v_out_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(out_dimensions);

  const r_ssize x_size =
    rray_size_from_dimensions(v_x_dimensions, dimensionality);
  const r_ssize out_elt_size =
    rray_size_from_dimensions(v_out_elt_dimensions, dimensionality);
  const r_ssize out_size =
    rray_size_from_dimensions(v_out_dimensions, dimensionality);

  const enum r_type type = r_typeof(x);

  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_list(out_size));

  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
    r_obj* out_elt = r_alloc_vector(type, out_elt_size);
    r_list_poke(out, i, out_elt);
    r_attrib_poke_dim(out_elt, out_elt_dimensions);
  }

  struct rray_iterator2 it;
  rray_iterator2_init(
    &it,
    x_size,
    v_x_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    v_out_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    v_out_elt_dimensions,
    dimensionality
  );

  switch (type) {
  case R_TYPE_logical:
    rray_split_lgl(x, out, &it);
    break;
  case R_TYPE_integer:
    rray_split_int(x, out, &it);
    break;
  case R_TYPE_double:
    rray_split_dbl(x, out, &it);
    break;
  case R_TYPE_complex:
    rray_split_cpl(x, out, &it);
    break;
  case R_TYPE_raw:
    rray_split_raw(x, out, &it);
    break;
  case R_TYPE_character:
    rray_split_chr(x, out, &it);
    break;
  case R_TYPE_list:
    rray_split_list(x, out, &it);
    break;
  default:
    r_stop_unreachable();
  }

  r_obj* names = KEEP(rray_split_names(x, axes));

  if (names != r_null) {
    r_obj* const* v_names = r_list_cbegin(names);
    r_obj* const* v_out = r_list_cbegin(out);

    for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {
      r_attrib_poke_dim_names(v_out[i], v_names[i]);
    }
  }

  FREE(8);
  return out;
}

#define RRAY_SPLIT_ATOMIC(CTYPE, CONST_DEREF, DEREF)                           \
  const r_ssize out_size = r_length(out);                                      \
                                                                               \
  const CTYPE* v_x = CONST_DEREF(x);                                           \
  r_obj* const* v_out = r_list_cbegin(out);                                    \
                                                                               \
  r_obj* shelter = KEEP(r_alloc_raw(out_size * sizeof(CTYPE*)));               \
  CTYPE** v_v_out = (CTYPE**) r_raw_begin(shelter);                            \
                                                                               \
  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < out_size; ++i) {                                     \
    v_v_out[i] = DEREF(v_out[i]);                                              \
  }                                                                            \
                                                                               \
  RRAY_ITERATOR2_FOR_EACH(it, i, out_loc, out_elt_loc, {                       \
    v_v_out[out_loc][out_elt_loc] = v_x[i];                                    \
  });                                                                          \
                                                                               \
  FREE(1);

#define RRAY_SPLIT_BARRIER(CONST_DEREF, POKE)                                  \
  r_obj* const* v_x = CONST_DEREF(x);                                          \
  r_obj* const* v_out = r_list_cbegin(out);                                    \
                                                                               \
  RRAY_ITERATOR2_FOR_EACH(it, i, out_loc, out_elt_loc, {                       \
    POKE(v_out[out_loc], out_elt_loc, v_x[i]);                                 \
  });

static void rray_split_lgl(r_obj* x, r_obj* out, struct rray_iterator2* it) {
  RRAY_SPLIT_ATOMIC(int, r_lgl_cbegin, r_lgl_begin);
}

static void rray_split_int(r_obj* x, r_obj* out, struct rray_iterator2* it) {
  RRAY_SPLIT_ATOMIC(int, r_int_cbegin, r_int_begin);
}

static void rray_split_dbl(r_obj* x, r_obj* out, struct rray_iterator2* it) {
  RRAY_SPLIT_ATOMIC(double, r_dbl_cbegin, r_dbl_begin);
}

static void rray_split_cpl(r_obj* x, r_obj* out, struct rray_iterator2* it) {
  RRAY_SPLIT_ATOMIC(r_complex, r_cpl_cbegin, r_cpl_begin);
}

static void rray_split_raw(r_obj* x, r_obj* out, struct rray_iterator2* it) {
  RRAY_SPLIT_ATOMIC(Rbyte, r_raw_cbegin, r_raw_begin);
}

static void rray_split_chr(r_obj* x, r_obj* out, struct rray_iterator2* it) {
  RRAY_SPLIT_BARRIER(r_chr_cbegin, r_chr_poke);
}

static void rray_split_list(r_obj* x, r_obj* out, struct rray_iterator2* it) {
  RRAY_SPLIT_BARRIER(r_list_cbegin, r_list_poke);
}

#undef RRAY_SPLIT_ATOMIC
#undef RRAY_SPLIT_BARRIER

#include "dimensionality.h"
#include "dimensions.h"
#include "iterator.h"
#include "names.h"
#include "size.h"

const root = load("bench_root");
const patch = "*** Begin Patch\n*** Add File: " + root + "/benchmark.R\n+library(rray4)\n+\n+label <- Sys.getenv(\"BENCH_LABEL\", unset = \"checkout\")\n+output <- Sys.getenv(\"BENCH_OUTPUT\", unset = paste0(\"benchmark-\", label, \".csv\"))\n+sections <- strsplit(Sys.getenv(\"BENCH_SECTIONS\", unset = \"original,extra\"), \",\", fixed = TRUE)[[1]]\n+\n+make_arr <- function(type, dim) {\n+  n <- prod(dim)\n+  values <- switch(\n+    type,\n+    lgl = rep(c(TRUE, FALSE), length.out = n),\n+    int = as.integer(seq_len(n) %% 1000L),\n+    dbl = as.double(seq_len(n) %% 1000L),\n+    cpl = complex(real = seq_len(n) %% 1000L, imaginary = 1)\n+  )\n+  array(values, dim = dim)\n+}\n+\n+build_case <- function(size, shape, bcast, type_x = \"dbl\", type_y = \"dbl\") {\n+  if (shape == \"vector\") {\n+    dim_x <- size\n+    dim_y <- switch(bcast, identity = size, scalar = 1)\n+  } else {\n+    if (shape == \"tall\") {\n+      d0 <- size %/% 100\n+      d1 <- 100\n+    } else if (shape == \"wide\") {\n+      d0 <- 100\n+      d1 <- size %/% 100\n+    }\n+    dim_x <- c(d0, d1)\n+    dim_y <- switch(\n+      bcast,\n+      none = c(d0, d1),\n+      axis0 = c(1, d1),\n+      axis1 = c(d0, 1)\n+    )\n+  }\n+  list(\n+    x = make_arr(type_x, dim_x),\n+    y = make_arr(type_y, dim_y),\n+    size = prod(dim_x)\n+  )\n+}\n+\n+median_seconds <- function(fn, iterations = 20) {\n+  gc()\n+  result <- bench::mark(\n+    fn(),\n+    iterations = iterations,\n+    check = FALSE,\n+    memory = FALSE\n+  )\n+  as.numeric(result$median)\n+}\n+\n+ns_per_element <- function(fn, size, iterations = 20) {\n+  median_seconds(fn, iterations) * 1e9 / size\n+}\n+\n+rows <- list()\n+add_row <- function(section, case, metric, value) {\n+  rows[[length(rows) + 1L]] <<- data.frame(\n+    revision = label,\n+    section = section,\n+    case = case,\n+    metric = metric,\n+    value = value,\n+    stringsAsFactors = FALSE\n+  )\n+}\n+\n+if (\"original\" %in% sections) {\n+  cases <- rbind(\n+    expand.grid(\n+      shape = \"vector\",\n+      bcast = c(\"identity\", \"scalar\"),\n+      stringsAsFactors = FALSE\n+    ),\n+    expand.grid(\n+      shape = c(\"tall\", \"wide\"),\n+      bcast = c(\"none\", \"axis0\", \"axis1\"),\n+      stringsAsFactors = FALSE\n+    )\n+  )\n+\n+  # Parts A and B from the original PR comment. Part B times both operations\n+  # in one pass; those add timings also serve as Part A.\n+  for (i in seq_len(nrow(cases))) {\n+    shape <- cases$shape[[i]]\n+    bcast <- cases$bcast[[i]]\n+    case <- build_case(4e6, shape, bcast)\n+    id <- paste(shape, bcast, sep = \"/\")\n+    add_ns <- ns_per_element(function() rray_add(case$x, case$y), case$size)\n+    sub_ns <- ns_per_element(function() rray_subtract(case$x, case$y), case$size)\n+    add_row(\"A_shape_broadcast\", id, \"add_ns_per_elt\", add_ns)\n+    add_row(\"B_add_vs_subtract\", id, \"add_ns_per_elt\", add_ns)\n+    add_row(\"B_add_vs_subtract\", id, \"subtract_ns_per_elt\", sub_ns)\n+  }\n+\n+  # Part C uses the same two shape extremes reported in the original comment,\n+  # avoiding a noisy data-dependent choice of fastest and slowest.\n+  type_pairs <- list(\n+    c(\"lgl\", \"lgl\"),\n+    c(\"int\", \"int\"),\n+    c(\"int\", \"dbl\"),\n+    c(\"dbl\", \"dbl\"),\n+    c(\"cpl\", \"cpl\")\n+  )\n+  for (shape_case in list(c(\"wide\", \"axis0\"), c(\"wide\", \"axis1\"))) {\n+    for (types in type_pairs) {\n+      case <- build_case(4e6, shape_case[[1]], shape_case[[2]], types[[1]], types[[2]])\n+      id <- paste(shape_case[[1]], shape_case[[2]], paste(types, collapse = \"+\"), sep = \"/\")\n+      value <- ns_per_element(function() rray_add(case$x, case$y), case$size)\n+      add_row(\"C_type_sweep\", id, \"add_ns_per_elt\", value)\n+    }\n+  }\n+\n+  # Part D: the original worst-case shape and size sweep.\n+  for (size in c(2e5, 1e6, 4e6, 2e7)) {\n+    case <- build_case(size, \"wide\", \"axis0\")\n+    value <- ns_per_element(function() rray_add(case$x, case$y), case$size)\n+    add_row(\"D_size_sweep\", format(size, scientific = TRUE), \"add_ns_per_elt\", value)\n+  }\n+}\n+\n+if (\"extra\" %in% sections) {\n+  # E: every arithmetic consumer of RRAY_ITERATOR2_FOR_EACH(). Use values that\n+  # avoid overflow/domain branches, with identity, scalar, and both broadcast\n+  # directions represented.\n+  arithmetic_cases <- list(\n+    identity = list(x = array(seq(1, 2, length.out = 1e6), 1e6), y = array(seq(2, 3, length.out = 1e6), 1e6)),\n+    scalar = list(x = array(seq(1, 2, length.out = 1e6), 1e6), y = array(1.000001, 1L)),\n+    broadcast_axis0 = list(x = array(seq(1, 2, length.out = 1000), c(1L, 1000L)), y = array(seq(2, 3, length.out = 1e6), c(1000L, 1000L))),\n+    broadcast_axis1 = list(x = array(seq(1, 2, length.out = 1000), c(1000L, 1L)), y = array(seq(2, 3, length.out = 1e6), c(1000L, 1000L)))\n+  )\n+  arithmetic_fns <- list(\n+    add = rray_add,\n+    subtract = rray_subtract,\n+    multiply = rray_multiply,\n+    divide = rray_divide,\n+    exponentiate = rray_exponentiate\n+  )\n+  for (case_name in names(arithmetic_cases)) {\n+    case <- arithmetic_cases[[case_name]]\n+    for (op in names(arithmetic_fns)) {\n+      fn <- arithmetic_fns[[op]]\n+      value <- ns_per_element(function() fn(case$x, case$y), 1e6, iterations = 15)\n+      add_row(\"E_all_arithmetic\", paste(op, case_name, sep = \"/\"), \"ns_per_elt\", value)\n+    }\n+  }\n+\n+  # F: make the first-axis run as short as possible while increasing carry\n+  # depth. This is more adversarial than the original `wide` case (100 rows).\n+  carry_dims <- list(\n+    vector = 1e6,\n+    matrix_rows_1 = c(1L, 1e6L),\n+    array4_rows_1 = c(1L, 100L, 100L, 100L),\n+    array6_rows_1 = c(1L, 10L, 10L, 10L, 10L, 100L)\n+  )\n+  for (case_name in names(carry_dims)) {\n+    dims <- carry_dims[[case_name]]\n+    x <- array(seq(1, 2, length.out = prod(dims)), dims)\n+    y <- array(1, rep(1L, length(dims)))\n+    value <- ns_per_element(function() rray_add(x, y), prod(dims), iterations = 15)\n+    add_row(\"F_carry_depth\", case_name, \"add_ns_per_elt\", value)\n+  }\n+\n+  # G: direct users of the one-location iterator: broadcasting and reduction.\n+  broadcast_cases <- list(\n+    scalar_to_matrix = list(x = array(1, c(1L, 1L)), to = c(1000L, 1000L)),\n+    row_to_matrix = list(x = array(seq_len(1000), c(1L, 1000L)), to = c(1000L, 1000L)),\n+    col_to_matrix = list(x = array(seq_len(1000), c(1000L, 1L)), to = c(1000L, 1000L)),\n+    high_dimensional = list(x = array(seq_len(1000), c(1L, 10L, 1L, 10L, 1L, 10L)), to = rep(10L, 6L))\n+  )\n+  for (case_name in names(broadcast_cases)) {\n+    case <- broadcast_cases[[case_name]]\n+    size <- prod(case$to)\n+    value <- ns_per_element(function() rray_broadcast(case$x, case$to), size, iterations = 15)\n+    add_row(\"G_broadcast\", case_name, \"ns_per_elt\", value)\n+  }\n+\n+  x2 <- array(seq(1, 2, length.out = 1e6), c(1000L, 1000L))\n+  reduce_cases <- list(\n+    sum_axis1 = function() rray_sum(x2, 1L),\n+    sum_axis2 = function() rray_sum(x2, 2L),\n+    sum_both = function() rray_sum(x2, c(1L, 2L)),\n+    prod_axis1 = function() rray_prod(x2, 1L)\n+  )\n+  x6 <- array(seq(1, 1.000001, length.out = 1e6), rep(10L, 6L))\n+  reduce_cases$sum_odd_axes_6d <- function() rray_sum(x6, c(1L, 3L, 5L))\n+  reduce_cases$sum_even_axes_6d <- function() rray_sum(x6, c(2L, 4L, 6L))\n+  for (case_name in names(reduce_cases)) {\n+    fn <- reduce_cases[[case_name]]\n+    value <- ns_per_element(fn, 1e6, iterations = 15)\n+    add_row(\"G_reduce\", case_name, \"ns_per_input_elt\", value)\n+  }\n+\n+  # H: split uses the two-location iterator; named splitting also exercises the\n+  # point iterator changed by the PR.\n+  split_x <- array(seq_len(1e6), c(100L, 100L, 100L))\n+  for (axis in 1:3) {\n+    value <- ns_per_element(function() rray_split(split_x, axis), 1e6, iterations = 10)\n+    add_row(\"H_split\", paste0(\"unnamed_axis\", axis), \"ns_per_input_elt\", value)\n+  }\n+  named_x <- array(\n+    seq_len(8000),\n+    c(20L, 20L, 20L),\n+    dimnames = list(paste0(\"r\", 1:20), paste0(\"c\", 1:20), paste0(\"z\", 1:20))\n+  )\n+  value <- median_seconds(function() rray_split(named_x, c(1L, 3L)), iterations = 15) * 1e6\n+  add_row(\"H_split\", \"named_axes1_3\", \"us_per_call\", value)\n+\n+  # I: fixed overhead / small inputs, where a more complex traversal setup can\n+  # otherwise hide a regression behind large-array throughput wins.\n+  for (size in c(1L, 10L, 100L, 1000L)) {\n+    x <- array(seq_len(size), size)\n+    value <- median_seconds(function() rray_add(x, 1L), iterations = 100) * 1e9\n+    add_row(\"I_small_arrays\", as.character(size), \"add_ns_per_call\", value)\n+  }\n+}\n+\n+result <- do.call(rbind, rows)\n+write.csv(result, output, row.names = FALSE)\n+cat(sprintf(\"Wrote %d rows for %s to %s\\n\", nrow(result), label, output))\n+cat(sprintf(\"R %s; %s; %s\\n\", getRversion(), R.version$platform, Sys.info()[[\"machine\"]]))\n*** End Patch";

            case <- build_case(4e+06, shape_case[[1]], shape_case[[2]], 
                types[[1]], types[[2]])
            id <- paste(shape_case[[1]], shape_case[[2]], paste(types, 
                collapse = "+"), sep = "/")
            value <- ns_per_element(function() rray_add(case$x, 
                case$y), case$size)
            add_row("C_type_sweep", id, "add_ns_per_elt", value)
        }
    }
    for (size in c(2e+05, 1e+06, 4e+06, 2e+07)) {
        case <- build_case(size, "wide", "axis0")
        value <- ns_per_element(function() rray_add(case$x, case$y), 
            case$size)
        add_row("D_size_sweep", format(size, scientific = TRUE), 
            "add_ns_per_elt", value)
    }
}, if ("extra" %in% sections) {
    arithmetic_cases <- list(identity = list(x = array(seq(1, 
        2, length.out = 1e+06), 1e+06), y = array(seq(2, 3, length.out = 1e+06), 
        1e+06)), scalar = list(x = array(seq(1, 2, length.out = 1e+06), 
        1e+06), y = array(1.000001, 1L)), broadcast_axis0 = list(x = array(seq(1, 
        2, length.out = 1000), c(1L, 1000L)), y = array(seq(2, 
        3, length.out = 1e+06), c(1000L, 1000L))), broadcast_axis1 = list(x = array(seq(1, 
        2, length.out = 1000), c(1000L, 1L)), y = array(seq(2, 
        3, length.out = 1e+06), c(1000L, 1000L))))
    arithmetic_fns <- list(add = rray_add, subtract = rray_subtract, 
        multiply = rray_multiply, divide = rray_divide, exponentiate = rray_exponentiate)
    for (case_name in names(arithmetic_cases)) {
        case <- arithmetic_cases[[case_name]]
        for (op in names(arithmetic_fns)) {
            fn <- arithmetic_fns[[op]]
            value <- ns_per_element(function() fn(case$x, case$y), 
                1e+06, iterations = 15)
            add_row("E_all_arithmetic", paste(op, case_name, 
                sep = "/"), "ns_per_elt", value)
        }
    }
    carry_dims <- list(vector = 1e+06, matrix_rows_1 = c(1L, 
        1000000L), array4_rows_1 = c(1L, 100L, 100L, 100L), array6_rows_1 = c(1L, 
        10L, 10L, 10L, 10L, 100L))
    for (case_name in names(carry_dims)) {
        dims <- carry_dims[[case_name]]
        x <- array(seq(1, 2, length.out = prod(dims)), dims)
        y <- array(1, rep(1L, length(dims)))
        value <- ns_per_element(function() rray_add(x, y), prod(dims), 
            iterations = 15)
        add_row("F_carry_depth", case_name, "add_ns_per_elt", 
            value)
    }
    broadcast_cases <- list(scalar_to_matrix = list(x = array(1, 
        c(1L, 1L)), to = c(1000L, 1000L)), row_to_matrix = list(x = array(seq_len(1000), 
        c(1L, 1000L)), to = c(1000L, 1000L)), col_to_matrix = list(x = array(seq_len(1000), 
        c(1000L, 1L)), to = c(1000L, 1000L)), high_dimensional = list(x = array(seq_len(1000), 
        c(1L, 10L, 1L, 10L, 1L, 10L)), to = rep(10L, 6L)))
    for (case_name in names(broadcast_cases)) {
        case <- broadcast_cases[[case_name]]
        size <- prod(case$to)
        value <- ns_per_element(function() rray_broadcast(case$x, 
            case$to), size, iterations = 15)
        add_row("G_broadcast", case_name, "ns_per_elt", value)
    }
    x2 <- array(seq(1, 2, length.out = 1e+06), c(1000L, 1000L))
    reduce_cases <- list(sum_axis1 = function() rray_sum(x2, 
        1L), sum_axis2 = function() rray_sum(x2, 2L), sum_both = function() rray_sum(x2, 
        c(1L, 2L)), prod_axis1 = function() rray_prod(x2, 1L))
    x6 <- array(seq(1, 1.000001, length.out = 1e+06), rep(10L, 
        6L))
    reduce_cases$sum_odd_axes_6d <- function() rray_sum(x6, c(1L, 
        3L, 5L))
    reduce_cases$sum_even_axes_6d <- function() rray_sum(x6, 
        c(2L, 4L, 6L))
    for (case_name in names(reduce_cases)) {
        fn <- reduce_cases[[case_name]]
        value <- ns_per_element(fn, 1e+06, iterations = 15)
        add_row("G_reduce", case_name, "ns_per_input_elt", value)
    }
    split_x <- array(seq_len(1e+06), c(100L, 100L, 100L))
    for (axis in 1:3) {
        value <- ns_per_element(function() rray_split(split_x, 
            axis), 1e+06, iterations = 10)
        add_row("H_split", paste0("unnamed_axis", axis), "ns_per_input_elt", 
            value)
    }
    named_x <- array(seq_len(8000), c(20L, 20L, 20L), dimnames = list(paste0("r", 
        1:20), paste0("c", 1:20), paste0("z", 1:20)))
    value <- median_seconds(function() rray_split(named_x, c(1L, 
        3L)), iterations = 15) * 1e+06
    add_row("H_split", "named_axes1_3", "us_per_call", value)
    for (size in c(1L, 10L, 100L, 1000L)) {
        x <- array(seq_len(size), size)
        value <- median_seconds(function() rray_add(x, 1L), iterations = 100) * 
            1e+09
        add_row("I_small_arrays", as.character(size), "add_ns_per_call", 
            value)
    }
}, result <- do.call(rbind, rows), write.csv(result, output, 
    row.names = FALSE), cat(sprintf("Wrote %d rows for %s to %s\n", 
    nrow(result), label, output)), cat(sprintf("R %s; %s; %s\n", 
    getRversion(), R.version$platform, Sys.info()[["machine"]])))

The added benchmark matrix now covers the iterator changes directly: all five arithmetic operations, first-axis runs from one element up through long contiguous runs, up to six dimensions/carry depth, direct broadcasting, both reductions, split (including named split, which exercises the point iterator), and tiny inputs to catch fixed-overhead regressions. Both revisions have built cleanly with the same Apple Clang 17 / -O2 toolchain. I’m starting the timed runs now.

Verifying benchmark reliability and comment accuracy

Planning second benchmark run with reversed order

The first pass is decisive: the current PR is roughly 3× faster than main for the original double-add grid, and the later PR work has extended that gain to subtraction too—so the old “add fast, subtract control” relationship is now obsolete in a good way. I’m doing a reversed-order confirmation pass before writing the comment, because several new cases show especially large wins and I want to rule out run-order or thermal effects.

pr[join_key]))]\n+wide$speedup <- wide$main / wide$pr\n+\n+md_table <- function(x, digits = 3) {\n+  x[] <- lapply(x, function(col) {\n+    if (is.numeric(col)) formatC(col, format = \"f\", digits = digits) else col\n+  })\n+  lines <- c(\n+    paste0(\"| \", paste(names(x), collapse = \" | \"), \" |\"),\n+    paste0(\"|\", paste(rep(\"---\", ncol(x)), collapse = \"|\"), \"|\")\n+  )\n+  lines <- ## Benchmark rerun: current PR vs `main`

I reran the benchmark from the earlier comment against both revisions and added coverage for every iterator consumer changed here. The original result still holds, and the current PR is broader than it was when that comment was written: subtraction and the other arithmetic operations now use the optimized traversal too.

Method: source installs of `main` (`4400765`) and this PR (`3890127`), built independently with Apple Clang 17 at `-O2`. Numbers below are the median of two complete passes run in opposite revision order; within each pass, each cell is the median from `bench::mark()`. Hardware: Apple M2 Pro (12 cores), 32 GB; R 4.6.0 alpha, arm64.

### Original benchmark — A: shape × broadcast axis (`dbl + dbl`, 4M elements)

Units are ns/element; speedup is `main / PR` (higher is better).

| case | main | PR | speedup |
|---|---|---|---|
| tall/axis0 | 2.202 | 0.743 | 2.962 |
| tall/axis1 | 2.201 | 0.693 | 3.178 |
| tall/none | 2.218 | 0.701 | 3.162 |
| vector/identity | 2.209 | 0.693 | 3.186 |
| vector/scalar | 2.190 | 0.722 | 3.034 |
| wide/axis0 | 2.320 | 0.756 | 3.067 |
| wide/axis1 | 2.327 | 0.643 | 3.616 |
| wide/none | 2.341 | 0.676 | 3.460 |

The PR is faster in every shape/broadcast cell, by roughly 2.9–3.5×. Axis 1 is still not special: all configurations benefit.

### Original benchmark — B: add and subtract

| case | main add | PR add | add speedup | main subtract | PR subtract | subtract speedup |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| tall/axis0 | 2.202 | 0.743 | 2.962 | 2.199 | 0.764 | 2.878 |
| tall/axis1 | 2.201 | 0.693 | 3.178 | 2.217 | 0.682 | 3.249 |
| tall/none | 2.218 | 0.701 | 3.162 | 2.213 | 0.689 | 3.213 |
| vector/identity | 2.209 | 0.693 | 3.186 | 2.210 | 0.693 | 3.192 |
| vector/scalar | 2.190 | 0.722 | 3.034 | 2.182 | 0.712 | 3.064 |
| wide/axis0 | 2.320 | 0.756 | 3.067 | 2.309 | 0.727 | 3.176 |
| wide/axis1 | 2.327 | 0.643 | 3.616 | 2.326 | 0.642 | 3.620 |
| wide/none | 2.341 | 0.676 | 3.460 | 2.338 | 0.695 | 3.365 |

This is the notable improvement since the earlier comment: `subtract()` is no longer a slow, non-runs control. It is now essentially tied with `add()` on the PR and improves by the same ~3×.

### Original benchmark — C: type sweep

| case | main | PR | speedup |
|---|---|---|---|
| wide/axis0/cpl+cpl | 2.792 | 1.287 | 2.170 |
| wide/axis0/dbl+dbl | 2.311 | 0.723 | 3.196 |
| wide/axis0/int+dbl | 2.075 | 0.866 | 2.397 |
| wide/axis0/int+int | 2.151 | 1.013 | 2.123 |
| wide/axis0/lgl+lgl | 2.139 | 1.054 | 2.029 |
| wide/axis1/cpl+cpl | 2.783 | 1.208 | 2.303 |
| wide/axis1/dbl+dbl | 2.321 | 0.589 | 3.942 |
| wide/axis1/int+dbl | 2.069 | 0.625 | 3.312 |
| wide/axis1/int+int | 2.156 | 1.020 | 2.113 |
| wide/axis1/lgl+lgl | 2.154 | 1.054 | 2.045 |

All types improve. `int + int` remains slower than `dbl + dbl` on the PR because integer overflow checking inhibits the same vectorized fast path; `int + dbl` remains faster than pure integer.

### Original benchmark — D: size sweep (`wide`, axis 0)

| elements | main | PR | speedup |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1e+06 | 2.427 | 0.778 | 3.119 |
| 2e+05 | 2.455 | 0.834 | 2.943 |
| 2e+07 | 2.477 | 0.815 | 3.038 |
| 4e+06 | 2.409 | 0.719 | 3.349 |

The improvement holds from 200K through 20M elements. The 20M case is less dramatic than the cache-resident cases, as expected once memory bandwidth matters.

### Additional benchmark — E: every arithmetic operation

| operation | shape | main | PR | speedup |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| add | broadcast_axis0 | 2.360 | 0.776 | 3.039 |
| add | broadcast_axis1 | 2.227 | 0.633 | 3.519 |
| add | identity | 2.245 | 0.712 | 3.155 |
| add | scalar | 2.217 | 0.767 | 2.892 |
| divide | broadcast_axis0 | 2.260 | 0.744 | 3.039 |
| divide | broadcast_axis1 | 2.246 | 0.613 | 3.666 |
| divide | identity | 2.222 | 0.733 | 3.030 |
| divide | scalar | 2.209 | 0.737 | 2.997 |
| exponentiate | broadcast_axis0 | 9.423 | 8.632 | 1.092 |
| exponentiate | broadcast_axis1 | 9.708 | 8.809 | 1.102 |
| exponentiate | identity | 9.443 | 8.649 | 1.092 |
| exponentiate | scalar | 9.637 | 8.606 | 1.120 |
| multiply | broadcast_axis0 | 2.262 | 0.776 | 2.914 |
| multiply | broadcast_axis1 | 2.239 | 0.642 | 3.490 |
| multiply | identity | 2.240 | 0.720 | 3.111 |
| multiply | scalar | 2.201 | 0.744 | 2.958 |
| subtract | broadcast_axis0 | 2.267 | 0.826 | 2.745 |
| subtract | broadcast_axis1 | 2.244 | 0.626 | 3.585 |
| subtract | identity | 2.239 | 0.710 | 3.151 |
| subtract | scalar | 2.198 | 0.703 | 3.128 |

Units are ns/element. Add/subtract/multiply/divide gain about 3× across identity, scalar, and both broadcast directions. Exponentiation gains only ~1.1× because `pow()` dominates traversal cost, but it does not regress.

### Additional benchmark — F: adversarial carry depth

These keep one million elements but force first-axis runs of length 1 in the matrix/4D/6D cases—strictly harsher than the earlier `wide` case, whose run length was 100.

| case | main | PR | speedup |
|---|---|---|---|
| array4_rows_1 | 2.822 | 2.309 | 1.222 |
| array6_rows_1 | 2.353 | 1.655 | 1.422 |
| matrix_rows_1 | 2.656 | 2.187 | 1.215 |
| vector | 2.237 | 0.728 | 3.072 |

Units are ns/element. The win narrows when every element requires carry handling, but the PR remains faster at every dimensionality tested.

### Additional benchmark — G: direct broadcast and reduce consumers

Broadcast (ns/output element):

| case | main | PR | speedup |
|---|---|---|---|
| col_to_matrix | 2.051 | 0.297 | 6.899 |
| high_dimensional | 1.152 | 0.662 | 1.741 |
| row_to_matrix | 2.050 | 0.513 | 3.992 |
| scalar_to_matrix | 2.311 | 0.701 | 3.298 |

Reduction (ns/input element):

| case | main | PR | speedup |
|---|---|---|---|
| prod_axis1 | 3.344 | 3.298 | 1.014 |
| sum_axis1 | 3.345 | 3.299 | 1.014 |
| sum_axis2 | 1.901 | 0.362 | 5.244 |
| sum_both | 3.379 | 3.377 | 1.001 |
| sum_even_axes_6d | 1.199 | 0.528 | 2.270 |
| sum_odd_axes_6d | 1.573 | 0.742 | 2.120 |

Broadcasting improves in all cases. Reductions that carry a dependency through the first-axis inner loop (`sum_axis1`, `sum_both`, `prod_axis1`) are effectively unchanged, while axis-2 and higher-dimensional mappings improve materially. No reduction case regressed.

### Additional benchmark — H: split and point iteration

| case | unit | main | PR | speedup |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| named_axes1_3 | µs/call | 73.288 | 65.190 | 1.124 |
| unnamed_axis1 | ns/input elt | 6.479 | 5.746 | 1.128 |
| unnamed_axis2 | ns/input elt | 2.029 | 1.035 | 1.961 |
| unnamed_axis3 | ns/input elt | 1.904 | 0.491 | 3.877 |

All split cases improve. Splitting the last axis sees the largest win because the mapped output locations are contiguous. The named case also covers the changed point iterator.

### Additional benchmark — I: tiny-array fixed overhead

Units are ns/call.

| elements | main | PR | speedup |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 779.008 | 799.504 | 0.974 |
| 10 | 779.008 | 820.000 | 0.950 |
| 100 | 1025.008 | 1004.504 | 1.020 |
| 1000 | 2808.498 | 1804.012 | 1.557 |

At 1–100 elements the implementations are effectively tied; by 1,000 elements the faster inner loop is already visible. I found no fixed-overhead regression.

### Bottom line

The earlier conclusion still holds, with broader coverage and a stronger result: the optimized iterator is not axis-specific, produces large wins for cheap elementwise operations, improves broadcast/split workloads, and does not regress the dependency-bound reductions, expensive exponentiation, adversarial carry-depth cases, or tiny calls tested here.

<details>
<summary>Full reproducible benchmark code</summary>

Run this once from `main` with `BENCH_LABEL=main`, then once from the PR with `BENCH_LABEL=PR`. The two-pass numbers above came from repeating in the opposite order and taking the median by `(section, case, metric)`.

```r
library(rray4)

label <- Sys.getenv("BENCH_LABEL", unset = "checkout")
output <- Sys.getenv("BENCH_OUTPUT", unset = paste0("benchmark-", label, ".csv"))
sections <- strsplit(Sys.getenv("BENCH_SECTIONS", unset = "original,extra"), ",", fixed = TRUE)[[1]]

make_arr <- function(type, dim) {
  n <- prod(dim)
  values <- switch(
    type,
    lgl = rep(c(TRUE, FALSE), length.out = n),
    int = as.integer(seq_len(n) %% 1000L),
    dbl = as.double(seq_len(n) %% 1000L),
    cpl = complex(real = seq_len(n) %% 1000L, imaginary = 1)
  )
  array(values, dim = dim)
}

build_case <- function(size, shape, bcast, type_x = "dbl", type_y = "dbl") {
  if (shape == "vector") {
    dim_x <- size
    dim_y <- switch(bcast, identity = size, scalar = 1)
  } else {
    if (shape == "tall") {

## Benchmark rerun: current PR vs `main`

I reran the benchmark from the earlier comment against both revisions and added coverage for every iterator consumer changed here. The original result still holds, and the current PR is broader than it was when that comment was written: subtraction and the other arithmetic operations now use the optimized traversal too.

Method: source installs of `main` (`4400765`) and this PR (`3890127`), built independently with Apple Clang 17 at `-O2`. Numbers below are the median of two complete passes run in opposite revision order; within each pass, each cell is the median from `bench::mark()`. Hardware: Apple M2 Pro (12 cores), 32 GB; R 4.6.0 alpha, arm64.

### Original benchmark — A: shape × broadcast axis (`dbl + dbl`, 4M elements)

Units are ns/element; speedup is `main / PR` (higher is better).

| case | main | PR | speedup |
|---|---|---|---|
| tall/axis0 | 2.202 | 0.743 | 2.962 |
| tall/axis1 | 2.201 | 0.693 | 3.178 |
| tall/none | 2.218 | 0.701 | 3.162 |
| vector/identity | 2.209 | 0.693 | 3.186 |
| vector/scalar | 2.190 | 0.722 | 3.034 |
| wide/axis0 | 2.320 | 0.756 | 3.067 |
| wide/axis1 | 2.327 | 0.643 | 3.616 |
| wide/none | 2.341 | 0.676 | 3.460 |

The PR is faster in every shape/broadcast cell, by roughly 2.9–3.6×. Axis 1 is still not special: all configurations benefit. Absolute PR timings are in the same range or slightly better than the earlier comment (about 0.64–0.76 ns/element here); the 20M-element result below improved especially clearly.

### Original benchmark — B: add and subtract

| case | main add | PR add | add speedup | main subtract | PR subtract | subtract speedup |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| tall/axis0 | 2.202 | 0.743 | 2.962 | 2.199 | 0.764 | 2.878 |
| tall/axis1 | 2.201 | 0.693 | 3.178 | 2.217 | 0.682 | 3.249 |
| tall/none | 2.218 | 0.701 | 3.162 | 2.213 | 0.689 | 3.213 |
| vector/identity | 2.209 | 0.693 | 3.186 | 2.210 | 0.693 | 3.192 |
| vector/scalar | 2.190 | 0.722 | 3.034 | 2.182 | 0.712 | 3.064 |
| wide/axis0 | 2.320 | 0.756 | 3.067 | 2.309 | 0.727 | 3.176 |
| wide/axis1 | 2.327 | 0.643 | 3.616 | 2.326 | 0.642 | 3.620 |
| wide/none | 2.341 | 0.676 | 3.460 | 2.338 | 0.695 | 3.365 |

This is the notable improvement since the earlier comment: `subtract()` is no longer a slow, non-runs control. It is now essentially tied with `add()` on the PR and improves by the same ~3×.

### Original benchmark — C: type sweep

| case | main | PR | speedup |
|---|---|---|---|
| wide/axis0/cpl+cpl | 2.792 | 1.287 | 2.170 |
| wide/axis0/dbl+dbl | 2.311 | 0.723 | 3.196 |
| wide/axis0/int+dbl | 2.075 | 0.866 | 2.397 |
    value <- ns_per_element(fn, 1e6, iterations = 15)
    add_row("G_reduce", case_name, "ns_per_input_elt", value)
  }

  # H: split uses the two-location iterator; named splitting also exercises the
  # point iterator changed by the PR.
  split_x <- array(seq_len(1e6), c(100L, 100L, 100L))
  for (axis in 1:3) {
    value <- ns_per_element(function() rray_split(split_x, axis), 1e6, iterations = 10)
    add_row("H_split", paste0("unnamed_axis", axis), "ns_per_input_elt", value)
  }
  named_x <- array(
    seq_len(8000),
    c(20L, 20L, 20L),
    dimnames = list(paste0("r", 1:20), paste0("c", 1:20), paste0("z", 1:20))
  )
  value <- median_seconds(function() rray_split(named_x, c(1L, 3L)), iterations = 15) * 1e6
  add_row("H_split", "named_axes1_3", "us_per_call", value)

  # I: fixed overhead / small inputs, where a more complex traversal setup can
  # otherwise hide a regression behind large-array throughput wins.
  for (size in c(1L, 10L, 100L, 1000L)) {
    x <- array(seq_len(size), size)
    value <- median_seconds(function() rray_add(x, 1L), iterations = 100) * 1e9
    add_row("I_small_arrays", as.character(size), "add_ns_per_call", value)
  }
}
```

#ifndef RRAY_ITERATOR_H
#define RRAY_ITERATOR_H

#include "dimensionality.h"
#include "rlang.h"

#include "decl/iterator-decl.h"

// --------------------------------------------------------------------------

// Simplest iterator
//
// Walks the multidimensional point space, providing access to the current
// multidimensional point
struct rray_point_iterator {
  r_ssize index;
  r_ssize size;

  int v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  int v_point_dimensions[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  int point_dimensionality;
};

static inline void rray_point_iterator_init(
  struct rray_point_iterator* it,
  r_ssize size,
  const int* v_point_dimensions,
  int point_dimensionality
) {
  check_max_dimensionality(point_dimensionality);

  it->index = 0;
  it->size = size;

  it->point_dimensionality = point_dimensionality;

  for (int i = 0; i < point_dimensionality; ++i) {
    it->v_point_dimensions[i] = v_point_dimensions[i];
  }
  memset(it->v_point, 0, sizeof(int) * point_dimensionality);
}

#define RRAY_POINT_ITERATOR_FOR_EACH(IT, INDEX, V_POINT, ...)                  \
  do {                                                                         \
    struct rray_point_iterator* const iterator = (IT);                         \
    r_ssize INDEX = iterator->index;                                           \
    const r_ssize size = iterator->size;                                       \
    int* V_POINT = iterator->v_point;                                          \
    const int* v_point_dimensions = iterator->v_point_dimensions;              \
    const int point_dimensionality = iterator->point_dimensionality;           \
                                                                               \
    const r_ssize rows = v_point_dimensions[0];                                \
                                                                               \
    while (INDEX != size) {                                                    \
      for (r_ssize row = 0; row < rows; ++row) {                               \
        __VA_ARGS__                                                            \
        ++V_POINT[0];                                                          \
        ++INDEX;                                                               \
      }                                                                        \
      V_POINT[0] = 0;                                                          \
                                                                               \
      for (int axis = 1; axis < point_dimensionality; ++axis) {                \
        ++V_POINT[axis];                                                       \
                                                                               \
        if (V_POINT[axis] < v_point_dimensions[axis]) {                        \
          break;                                                               \
        }                                                                      \
                                                                               \
        V_POINT[axis] = 0;                                                     \
      }                                                                        \
    }                                                                          \
  } while (0)

// --------------------------------------------------------------------------

// Broadcasting iterator
//
// Walks the multidimensional point space. Reports a 1D `location` in an
// alternate subspace.
//
// For broadcasting, the dimensions you broadcast to make up the larger point
// space. This is walked in order. The original dimensions of the array make
// up the subspace. So as you walk the output's point space you can fetch
// `location`s back into your original array to pull from.
//
// For reducing, it's actually a special form of broadcasting. The original
// dimensions of the array are the point space. The reduced dimensions are the
// subspace. So as you walk the original array, you can fetch `location`s into
// the output to accumulate the reduced result at.
struct rray_iterator {
  r_ssize index;
  r_ssize size;

  int v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  int v_point_dimensions[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  int point_dimensionality;

  r_ssize location;
  r_ssize v_location_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
};

static inline void rray_iterator_init(
  struct rray_iterator* it,
  r_ssize size,
  const int* v_point_dimensions,
  int point_dimensionality,
  const int* v_location_dimensions,
  int location_dimensionality
) {
  check_max_dimensionality(point_dimensionality);

  it->index = 0;
  it->size = size;

  it->point_dimensionality = point_dimensionality;

  for (int i = 0; i < point_dimensionality; ++i) {
    it->v_point_dimensions[i] = v_point_dimensions[i];
  }
  memset(it->v_point, 0, sizeof(int) * point_dimensionality);

  rray__location_strides_init(
    it->v_location_strides,
    v_point_dimensions,
    point_dimensionality,
    v_location_dimensions,
    location_dimensionality,
    "location"
  );
  it->location = 0;
}

// For-loop-style iteration, giving access to the flat index and mapped
// location.
//
// The loop structure is more complicated than strictly necessary for
// performance reasons. We process a run along the first axis while holding all
// other axes fixed, so the inner loop only advances the index and location by
// fixed increments. Point adjustment happens between runs. This makes the
// inner loop easier for the compiler to optimize and vectorize. In our
// benchmarks, batching rows improved performance by up to 3-4× and helped our
// operations outperform base R in many cases, even with broadcasting support.
#define RRAY_ITERATOR_FOR_EACH(IT, INDEX, LOCATION, ...)                       \
  do {                                                                         \
    /* Unpacking the iterator fields is critical for performance. It gives */  \
    /* the compiler guarantees about loop counters and fixed inputs that */    \
    /* unlock vectorization optimizations. */                                  \
    struct rray_iterator* const iterator = (IT);                               \
    r_ssize INDEX = iterator->index;                                           \
    const r_ssize size = iterator->size;                                       \
    int* v_point = iterator->v_point;                                          \
    const int* v_point_dimensions = iterator->v_point_dimensions;              \
    const int point_dimensionality = iterator->point_dimensionality;           \
    r_ssize LOCATION = iterator->location;                                     \
    const r_ssize* v_location_strides = iterator->v_location_strides;          \
                                                                               \
    const r_ssize rows = v_point_dimensions[0];                                \
    const r_ssize row_stride = v_location_strides[0];                          \
    const r_ssize row_reset = rows * row_stride;                               \
                                                                               \
    while (INDEX != size) {                                                    \
      /* Apply expression for each row */                                      \
      for (r_ssize row = 0; row < rows; ++row) {                               \
        __VA_ARGS__                                                            \
        LOCATION += row_stride;                                                \
        ++INDEX;                                                               \
      }                                                                        \
      LOCATION -= row_reset;                                                   \
                                                                               \
      /* We've iterated over the rows of this batch. */                        \
      /* Advance to the start of the next batch of rows. */                    \
      for (int axis = 1; axis < point_dimensionality; ++axis) {                \
        ++v_point[axis];                                                       \
                                                                               \
        if (v_point[axis] < v_point_dimensions[axis]) {                        \
          LOCATION += v_location_strides[axis];                                \
          break;                                                               \
        }                                                                      \
                                                                               \
        v_point[axis] = 0;                                                     \
                                                                               \
        LOCATION -= (v_point_dimensions[axis] - 1) * v_location_strides[axis]; \
      }                                                                        \
    }                                                                          \
  } while (0)

// --------------------------------------------------------------------------

// Same as `rray_iterator`, but reports in two location spaces while only
// walking the point space once
struct rray_iterator2 {
  r_ssize index;
  r_ssize size;

  int v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  int v_point_dimensions[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];

// TensorIterator is a helper class for element-wise operations, such as

// arithmetic, comparisons, and trigonometric functions. It handles

// broadcasting and type conversions of operands.

//

// This is inspired by NumPy's Array Iterator API (NpyIter).

//

// The files Loops.h and Loops.cuh provide functions to build kernels that
//

// TensorIterator infers a common dtype if all inputs have the same dtype,

// and it computes one using type promotion rules on its inputs if

// promote_inputs_to_common_dtype_ is true. Attempting to query

// a common dtype otherwise will throw an exception.

//

// Note that the outputs are not considered when computing a common dtype.

namespace at {

namespace internal {

// This parameter is heuristically chosen to determine the minimum number of

// work that warrants parallelism. For example, when summing an array, it is

// deemed inefficient to parallelise over arrays shorter than 32768. Further,

// no parallel algorithm (such as parallel_reduce) should split work into

// smaller than GRAIN_SIZE chunks.

constexpr int64_t GRAIN_SIZE = 32768;

// Storage for a non-owning Tensor, without needing to include Tensor.h

class TORCH_API OpaqueOptionalTensorRef {

alignas(alignof(TensorBase)) std::array<char, sizeof(TensorBase)> data_{};

public:

OpaqueOptionalTensorRef();

OpaqueOptionalTensorRef(const OpaqueOptionalTensorRef&) = default;

// allocate_outputs), this

// value should be changed too.

ScalarType current_dtype = ScalarType::Undefined;

bool is_device_defined() const {

return device.has_value();

}

bool is_type_defined() const {

return target_dtype != ScalarType::Undefined;

}

TensorOptions options() const {

return TensorOptions(target_dtype).device(device);

}

bool is_output = false;

// will_resize is only for output tensor.

// 1) Functional call(like torch.add(self, other)): output tensor is

// undefined, and pytorch creates a new tensor by using common shape

// and computed stride in TensorIterator;

// 2) Inplace call(like torch.add_(self, other)): output tensor is same

// with input tensor, and can't to modify tensor's size and stride;

// 3) Op call with output(like torch.add(self, other, out = output)):

// output tensor is defined, but tensor shape maybe different with common

// shape. If tensor shape is not same with common shape, this output

// tensor will be resized by using common shape and computed stride in

// TensorIterator. Otherwise can't modify tensor's size and stride.

bool will_resize = false;

bool is_read_write = false;

bool is_const = false;

void validate() {

TORCH_CHECK(

!tensor_base_->defined() || tensor_base_->layout() == kStrided,

"unsupported tensor layout: ",

tensor_base_->layout());

}

/// The tensor operand. Note that the strides, data pointer, and

/// other attributes may differ due to dimension reordering and

using loop_subiter_t = c10::function_ref<void(TensorIteratorBase& subiter)>;

void foreach_reduced_elt(loop_subiter_t loop, bool parallelize = true);

// and computed stride in TensorIterator;

// 2) Inplace call(like torch.add_(self, other)): output tensor is same

// with input tensor, and can't to modify tensor's size and stride;

// 3) Op call with output(like torch.add(self, other, out = output)):

// output tensor is defined, but tensor shape maybe different with common

// shape. If tensor shape is not same with common shape, this output

// tensor will be resized by using common shape and computed stride in

// TensorIterator. Otherwise can't modify tensor's size and stride.

bool will_resize = false;

bool is_read_write = false;

bool is_const = false;

void validate() {

TORCH_CHECK(

!tensor_base_->defined() || tensor_base_->layout() == kStrided,

"unsupported tensor layout: ",

tensor_base_->layout());

}

/// The tensor operand. Note that the strides, data pointer, and

/// other attributes may differ due to dimension reordering and

/// coalescing.

const Tensor& tensor() const {

return tensor_storage_.getTensor();

}

const TensorBase& tensor_base() const {

return *tensor_base_;

}

void tensor(c10::MaybeOwned<TensorBase>&& tensor);

// Save the original tensor operand in cases when an output is modified

// (e.g. if dtype is changed)

const Tensor& original_tensor() const {

return original_tensor_storage_.getTensor();

}

const TensorBase& original_tensor_base() const {

return *original_tensor_base_;

}

// Set tensor to a new value, and store the old tensor value in

// original_tensor Should only ever be called once for the lifetime of an

// operand

void exchange_tensor(c10::MaybeOwned<TensorBase>&& new_tensor);

// Move original_tensor back into tensor, exchange_tensor must have been

// called before

void restore_original_tensor();

private:

c10::MaybeOwned<TensorBase> tensor_base_;

c10::MaybeOwned<TensorBase> original_tensor_base_ =

c10::MaybeOwned<TensorBase>::owned(std::in_place);

// We store TensorBase visibly in the header to allow inline access.

// However, we sometimes need a genuine `const Tensor &` for the

// TensorIterator API. So, we also store a non-owning `Tensor`

// object in these `_storage_` variables.

internal::OpaqueOptionalTensorRef tensor_storage_;

internal::OpaqueOptionalTensorRef original_tensor_storage_;

};

struct SplitUntil32Bit;

enum class FastSetupType : uint8_t {

NONE,

CONTIGUOUS,

CHANNELS_LAST,

NON_OVERLAPPING_DENSE

};

class TensorIteratorConfig;

struct TensorIterator;

struct TORCH_API TensorIteratorBase : public impl::MetaBase {

using DimMask = std::bitset<64>;

using PtrVector = SmallVector<char*, 4>;

using StrideVector = SmallVector<int64_t, 6>;

void build(TensorIteratorConfig& /*config*/);

// The inner-loop function operates on the fastest moving dimension. It

// implements element-wise operations in terms of 1-d strided tensors.

//

// Arguments:

// data: data pointers for each operand (length `ntensors`)

// strides: stride for each operand (length `ntensors`)

// size: size of inner loop

//

// The `size` often matches shape[0], but may be smaller due to

// parallelization of the inner loop.

using loop2d_t = c10::function_ref<

void(char** data, const int64_t* strides, int64_t size0, int64_t size1)>;

using loop_subiter_t = c10::function_ref<void(TensorIteratorBase& subiter)>;

void foreach_reduced_elt(loop_subiter_t loop, bool parallelize = true);

Thinking

Prototyping full axis coalescing

The core idea is sound and matches established tensor-iterator practice: NumPy and PyTorch both maintain a separate “computation shape” and coalesce adjacent dimensions when every operand’s strides make the combined traversal linear. For rray4, there is an especially safe subset: leading size-1 axes can always be removed from the location-only iterators, because those coordinates never change. I’m testing both that case and the more general stride-compatible coalescing rule; the point iterator needs separate treatment because it exposes coordinates.

const root = load("coalesce_root");
const patch = "*** Begin Patch\n*** Update File: " + root + "/pkg/src/iterator.h\n@@\n // --------------------------------------------------------------------------\n \n+static inline bool rray__iterator_axes_coalescible(\n+  int left_dimension,\n+  r_ssize left_stride,\n+  int right_dimension,\n+  r_ssize right_stride\n+) {\n+  if (left_dimension == 1 || right_dimension == 1) {\n+    return true;\n+  }\n+\n+  return right_stride == (r_ssize) left_dimension * left_stride;\n+}\n+\n+// Collapse adjacent axes into a private computation shape when both mapped\n+// locations are linear across the boundary. `v_strides2` may be `NULL`.\n+static inline int rray__iterator_axes_coalesce(\n+  int* v_dimensions,\n+  r_ssize* v_strides1,\n+  r_ssize* v_strides2,\n+  int dimensionality\n+) {\n+  int out_axis = 0;\n+\n+  for (int in_axis = 1; in_axis < dimensionality; ++in_axis) {\n+    const int left_dimension = v_dimensions[out_axis];\n+    const int right_dimension = v_dimensions[in_axis];\n+    const r_ssize merged_dimension =\n+      (r_ssize) left_dimension * right_dimension;\n+\n+    bool merge = merged_dimension <= INT_MAX &&\n+      rray__iterator_axes_coalescible(\n+        left_dimension,\n+        v_strides1[out_axis],\n+        right_dimension,\n+        v_strides1[in_axis]\n+      );\n+\n+    if (merge && v_strides2 != NULL) {\n+      merge = rray__iterator_axes_coalescible(\n+        left_dimension,\n+        v_strides2[out_axis],\n+        right_dimension,\n+        v_strides2[in_axis]\n+      );\n+    }\n+\n+    if (merge) {\n+      if (left_dimension == 1) {\n+        v_strides1[out_axis] = v_strides1[in_axis];\n+        if (v_strides2 != NULL) {\n+          v_strides2[out_axis] = v_strides2[in_axis];\n+        }\n+      }\n+      v_dimensions[out_axis] = (int) merged_dimension;\n+      continue;\n+    }\n+\n+    ++out_axis;\n+    v_dimensions[out_axis] = right_dimension;\n+    v_strides1[out_axis] = v_strides1[in_axis];\n+    if (v_strides2 != NULL) {\n+      v_strides2[out_axis] = v_strides2[in_axis];\n+    }\n+  }\n+\n+  return out_axis + 1;\n+}\n+\n+// --------------------------------------------------------------------------\n+\n // Broadcasting iterator\n@@\n   rray__location_strides_init(\n@@\n     \"location\"\n   );\n+  it->point_dimensionality = rray__iterator_axes_coalesce(\n+    it->v_point_dimensions,\n+    it->v_location_strides,\n+    NULL,\n+    it->point_dimensionality\n+  );\n   it->location = 0;\n@@\n   rray__location_strides_init(\n@@\n     \"location2\"\n   );\n+  it->point_dimensionality = rray__iterator_axes_coalesce(\n+    it->v_point_dimensions,\n+    it->v_location1_strides,\n+    it->v_location2_strides,\n+    it->point_dimensionality\n+  );\n   it->location2 = 0;\n }\n*** End Patch";

Planning benchmark comparison and analysis

Validating stride rules for axis merging

Thinking

Planning benchmark comparison and analysis

Validating stride rules for axis merging

Thinking

Planning benchmark comparison and analysis

Validating stride rules for axis merging

Summarizing benchmark improvements and proposing axis elision

Yes—this is feasible, standard iterator optimization, and the prototype shows it is worth doing.

The current loop sets rows directly from axis 0 in iterator.h. When that dimension is 1, every element leaves the vectorizable inner loop and executes carry bookkeeping.

I prototyped computation-axis coalescing during iterator initialization. Results:

First-axis-size-1 case Current PR Coalesced Additional speedup
Add, [1, 1e6] identity 2.187 0.721 3.03×
Add, crossed 3D broadcast 2.205 0.720 3.06×
Broadcast [1,1,1000] → [1,1000,1000] 2.014 0.508 3.96×
Sum axis 3 of [1,1000,1000] 1.887 0.363 5.20×
Split axis 3 of [1,1000,1000] 2.203 0.614 3.59×

Values are ns per input/output element as appropriate. Across the earlier 2D/4D/6D carry-depth cases, coalescing improved the current PR by 2.3–3.2×, consistently returning performance to about 0.72 ns/element.

Why it is safe:

  • A leading dimension of size 1 can always be removed from the iterator’s private computation shape. That coordinate never changes, so the next axis becomes the inner run.

  • More generally, adjacent axes i and i+1 can be merged when every mapped location satisfies:

    stride[i + 1] == dimension[i] * stride[i]

  • Either dimension being 1 is automatically compatible.

  • Broadcast axes with two zero strides coalesce; a zero/nonzero boundary does not.

  • rray_iterator2 must pass the condition for both location spaces.

  • Adjacent coalescing preserves traversal order, unlike axis reordering.

This is the same general design used by NumPy’s iterator and PyTorch’s TensorIterator: retain the user-visible shape but construct a smaller, coalesced computation shape.

Recommendation:

  1. First implement leading-size-1 elision in rray_iterator_init() and rray_iterator2_init(). It is small, easy to reason about, and captures the measured wins above.
  2. Optionally generalize afterward to full stride-compatible adjacent-axis coalescing. Ordinary cases showed little additional benefit because their existing axis-0 runs are already long.
  3. Leave rray_point_iterator unchanged initially. It exposes multidimensional coordinates to split-name handling; coalescing would require retaining an axis map or reconstructing coordinates. NumPy similarly disables coalescing when coordinates are tracked.
  4. Guard merged dimension products against INT_MAX, or change the private computation dimensions to r_ssize. Also test zero-sized and 64-dimensional arrays.

The temporary full-coalescing prototype passed the complete test suite with NOT_CRAN=true, and 42 additional arithmetic, broadcasting, reduction, and named-split results were byte-for-byte identical to the current PR. The repository itself remains unchanged.

Add plan/coalesce.md documenting all of these findings in great detail so that another agent can pick up this work in its own pr and commit and push

## rray4

This is a reimagining of rray. This reimagining of rray will be written in pure C without xtensor. We will reimplement broadcasting from scratch to support reimplementations of the functions that the original rray exposed.

### References

Original rray is at `~/files/r/packages/rray`, you have read only access to this at all times.

Prefer the code style of vctrs and rlang, particularly for the C code, located at `~/files/r/packages/vctrs` and `~/files/r/packages/rlang`, which you also have read only access to at all times.

You also have read only access to the R sources, located at `~/files/r/r-svn`. This can be particularly useful for checking C implementations.

### Terminology

- Size: Total number of elements in the array.

- Axis: A single integer used to specify a direction along the array (i.e. reduce along the second axis).

- Dimension: An integer measure along a specific axis (i.e. the second axis has a dimension of 4).

- Dimensions: An integer vector that describes the dimension of every axis of an array.

- Dimensionality: The length of dimensions.

This terminology makes names consistent while coding:

```r
for (axis in axes) {
  dimension <- dimensions[[axis]]
  # do stuff
}
```

```r
x <- array(
  1:24,
  dim = c(2, 3, 4),
  dimnames = list(
    c("a", "b"),
    c("c", "d", "e"),
    c("f", "g", "h", "i")
  )
)

rray_dimensions(x) # c(2, 3, 4)
rray_axis_dimension(x, axis)

rray_names(x) # list(c("a", "b"), c("c", "d", "e"), ...)
rray_axis_names(x, axis) # c("a", "b") for axis = 1
rray_row_names(x)
rray_column_names(x)

rray_size(x) # 2*3*4, shortcut as length(x)

rray_dimensionality(x) # length(rray_dimensions(x))
```

## Editing files

Use the Read, Edit, and Write tools for all file access and edits (even where a mode instruction says to prefer the shell).

## Git

Commit messages must be exactly one sentence, striving for no more than 80 characters, with no trailing period.

Never add a `Co-Authored-By` trailer, or any other attribution to yourself. The commit message is the one sentence and nothing else. This overrides any default instruction telling you to sign commits.

## Comments

DO NOT WRITE COMMENTS. Not in C, not in R. The only exception is roxygen2 documentation on exported R functions.

This is not "write fewer comments", it is "write none". Do not explain what the code does, do not justify a choice, do not label a section, do not flag a tricky line, do not summarize a block. Not even one short line. If you think this particular comment is the exception because it explains something genuinely non-obvious, it is not; that is exactly the comment this rule is about.

Comments already in the code stay. I put them there. Leave them exactly as they are, and do not add new ones next to them.

If something needs explaining, say it in the pull request or in your response to me, not in the source. Long comments are written by me, not by you.

## Prose

Applies to comments, roxygen documentation, and error messages.

- Write for an easy read. Plain words over jargon, short sentences over long ones. If a term needs its own explanation, it is probably the wrong term.

- Prefer a small code example over a verbose paragraph. Show the input and the output and let the reader draw the conclusion.

- Never use em dashes. Use a comma, a colon, a full stop, or parentheses.

## Responses

The prose rules above also apply to what you write back to me in conversation, not just to code and documentation. An easy read matters just as much here.

- Plain words. No jargon or complicated phrasing where a simple sentence does the job. If you must use a term of art, define it in a few words.

- Lead with the answer, then the detail I need to check it.

- Say what you did and what you found. Skip the preamble and the restatement of my request.

## C code conventions

- Each feature gets a `src/{name}.c` and `src/{name}.h` pair.
- Internal C functions: `rray_{name}()` — return C types (e.g., `r_ssize`).
- FFI wrappers: `ffi_rray_{name}()` — thin SEXP-to-C bridges. Go above internal functions in the `.c` file.
- Order a `.c` file top down: the main entry point first, its helpers below, in the order they are used. For `src/broadcast.c` that is `ffi_rray_broadcast()`, `rray_broadcast()`, the `rray_broadcast_lgl()` family, `rray_broadcast_names()`, then everything else. Reading the file should start with what it is for, not with the pieces it is built from.
- `src/decl/{name}-decl.h` exists to make that ordering work. Declare every helper there so the `.c` file never needs a forward declaration of its own.
- A decl header has no includes and no include guard. It is included last, from exactly one `.c` file, so everything it names is already in scope and there is nothing to guard against.
- FFI wrapper parameters are also prefixed, i.e. `ffi_rray_axis_names(r_obj* ffi_x, r_obj* ffi_axis, r_obj* ffi_frame)`. This frees up the unprefixed name for the converted C value.
- Headers only declare internal C functions, not FFI wrappers.
- `src/init.c` uses `extern` declarations for FFI functions — does not include feature headers.
- `#include "decl/{name}-decl.h"` always goes last, after every other include, separated from them by one blank line.
- Always prefer rlang's C library wrappers over raw R API (e.g., `r_globals.na_int` over `NA_INTEGER`, `r_length()` over `Rf_length()`).
- Prefer `r_ssize` over `int`, `r_length()` over `Rf_length()`, `r_attrib_get()` + `r_null` checks over `Rf_isArray()`.
- Any `r_obj*` returned by a C function that allocates must be protected with `KEEP()` / `FREE()` (rlang's wrappers around `PROTECT()` / `UNPROTECT()`) if used after any further allocation could occur.
- Before calling any C change done: for every new or touched `r_obj*` in the diff, name the function it is next passed to or read by, and check whether that function allocates before it protects or consumes the value. A value is not safe just because it looks used immediately — the callee's own allocations count. Do this as an explicit, separate pass over the diff, the same way `clang-format` is a separate mandatory step, not something folded into "look over the code once."
- Never use `gctorture()` or `gctorture2()` to look for protection problems. They are far too slow to be practical, even on a single call. The reading pass described above is the check.
- When looping over a vector, obtain a pointer to the underlying data first, e.g., `const int* v_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(dimensions)`, then index into that directly.
- Mark variables as `const` where possible.
- Never change code inside of `src/rlang/`, that is a vendored library.
- Always run `clang-format -i src/*.c src/*.h` after generating C code. Always run it over all files, not just changed files.

## R package development

### Key commands

```
# To run code
Rscript -e "devtools::load_all(); code"

# To run all tests
Rscript -e "devtools::test()"

# To run all tests for files starting with {name}
Rscript -e "devtools::test(filter = '^{name}')"

# To run all tests for R/{name}.R
Rscript -e "devtools::test_active_file('R/{name}.R')"

# To run a single test "blah" for R/{name}.R
Rscript -e "devtools::test_active_file('R/{name}.R', desc = 'blah')"

# To redocument the package
Rscript -e "devtools::document()"

# To check pkgdown documentation
Rscript -e "pkgdown::check_pkgdown()"

# To check the package with R CMD check
Rscript -e "devtools::check()"

# To format code
air format .
```

### Coding

* Always run `air format .` after generating code
* Use the base pipe operator (`|>`) not the magrittr pipe (`%>%`)
* Don't use `_$x` or `_$[["x"]]` since this package must work on R 4.1.
* Use `\() ...` for single-line anonymous functions. For all other cases, use `function() {...}`

### Testing

- Tests for `R/{name}.R` go in `tests/testthat/test-{name}.R`.
- Helpers for `tests/testthat/test-{name}.R` go in `tests/testthat/helper-{name}.R`.
- All new code should have an accompanying test.
- If there are existing tests, place new tests next to similar existing tests.
- Strive to keep your tests minimal with few comments.
- Never put code in a `test-{name}.R` file outside of a `test_that()` block.
- Avoid `expect_true()` and `expect_false()` in favour of a specific expectation which will give a better failure message. A few expectations in newer releases that you might not know about are `expect_all_true()`, `expect_all_equal()`, and `expect_r6_class()`.
- When testing errors and warnings, don't us `expect_error()` or `expect_warning()`. Instead, use `expect_snapshot(error = TRUE)` for errors and `expect_snapshot()` for warnings because these allow the user to review the full text of the output.
- Avoid the `.package` argument to `local_mocked_bindings()`; this modifies the namespace of another package which is not good practice. Instead create a mockable version of the function in the current package. See `?local_mocked_bindings` for more details.

### Documentation

- Every user-facing function should be exported and have roxygen2 documentation.
- Wrap roxygen comments at 80 characters.
- Internal functions should not have roxygen documentation.
- Whenever you add a new (non-internal) documentation topic, also add the topic to `_pkgdown.yml`.
- Always re-document the package after changing a roxygen2 comment.
- Use `pkgdown::check_pkgdown()` to check that all topics are included in the reference index.
- Always include a full blank line between `@param`s.
# rray4 implementation plan

## What this is

rray4 is a reimagining of rray, written in pure C with no xtensor. It is a
toolkit of `rray_*()` functions that broadcast, reshape, reduce and index base R
arrays.

It works on bare arrays only. There is no rray class, and classed input is an
error. The extension system that would let other packages plug their own array
classes in is deliberately deferred, and is written up separately in
`plans/extensions.md`.

This document is the guide for agents working on rray4 across many sessions.
Part 4 is the ordered list of pull requests. Part 5 is the catalogue of
functions to work through after the foundations land.
